Let's write a first candidate: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, group_size): super().__init__() torch.manual_seed(0) self.K = K self.N = N self.group_size = group_size num_groups = K // group_size # generate random fp16 weights w = torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda') # symmetric quantization to int4 [-8, 7] w = w.reshape(N, num_groups, group_size) wmax = w.abs().amax(dim=-1, keepdim=True) self.scales = (wmax / 7.0).squeeze(-1).to(torch.float16) # [N, num_groups] w_q = torch.clamp(torch.round(w / self.scales.unsqueeze(-1)), -8, 7).to(torch.int8) self.register_buffer('qweight', w_q.reshape(N, K)) self.register_buffer('scales_t', self.scales.t().contiguous()) # [num_groups, N] # bias? self.bias = torch.zeros(N, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda') def forward(self, a): # a: [M, K] M = a.shape[0] # dequantize weights to fp16 num_groups = self.K // self.group_size a_reshaped = a.reshape(M, num_groups, self.group_size) w_reshaped = self.qweight.reshape(self.N, num_groups, self.group_size).to(torch.float16) # matmul: for each group # y = a @ w.T # easier: dequantize full weight scales_expanded = self.scales_t.reshape(num_groups, 1, self.N).expand(num_groups, self.group_size, self.N) # Actually scales_t is [num_groups, N]. We need [num_groups, group_size, N] but same scale per group. w_deq = w_reshaped * self.scales_t.t().unsqueeze(-1) # [N, num_groups, group_size] * [N, num_groups, 1] w_deq = w_deq.reshape(self.N, self.K).t().contiguous() # [K, N] out = a @ w_deq # [M, N] return out + self.bias ``` Wait, `self.scales` shape. Wait, we could also use `torch.nn.functional.linear` with dequantized weights in forward, but the problem says custom CUDA. Let's write a simple kernel. CUDA kernel design: - Use 1D grid of blocks over N. Each block handles e.g. 128 N columns. - Threads per block: 128. - Each thread computes one output element for all M rows? Or each thread computes one (m,n) output. - Total outputs: M*N = 16*4096 = 65536. With 128 threads per block and 32 blocks (to cover 4096 N columns), each block handles 128 N columns * 16 M rows = 2048 outputs. Each thread handles 16 outputs (one per M row) for one N column. That's 16 dot products of length K per thread. - K=4096. Each dot product is 4096 multiplies and adds. That's a lot per thread. Better to parallelize K as well. - Alternative: each block handles a tile of N (e.g., 256) and a warp handles one M row. Use warp-level reduction over K. - Or: since A is small, load A into shared memory [M, K] (fp16). Then each thread Computes a few (m,n) outputs by iterating over K and reading B from global memory. Since B is quantized, reading int8 per element. Global memory access for B: each thread reads K * num_n_per_thread elements. If each thread reads 1 N column and 16 M rows, that's 4096 * 16 = 65536 int8 reads per thread. With 65536 threads total (128 threads * 32 blocks * 16 M? No, 128 threads * 32 blocks = 4096 threads. Each handles 16 outputs (one for each M). So 4096 threads * 4096 K reads = 16M reads. That might be okay on H100. - But we can do better: each thread handles a sub-tile of N and M. Let's assign each thread one (m,n) pair. Total threads needed = 65536. We can launch 65536 threads (e.g., 128 threads/block, 512 blocks). Each thread computes a dot product of length 4096. 4096 flop per thread. Total FLOPs = 2*M*N*K = 2*16*4096*4096 ≈ 536M. H100 can do that easily. Memory bandwidth: reading A (128KB) and B (8MB) and scales (32*4096*2=256KB). Total memory read per matmul ~8.4MB. At 3.35 TB/s, that's ~2.5 microseconds. But kernel overhead and dequantization might add. Still, a simple kernel could be very fast. But reading B as int8 per element is not coalesced if each thread reads a different n but same k? Actually, if threads in a warp have consecutive n, then for a given k, they read adjacent memory locations (since B is [N, K] row-major, B[n, k] for consecutive n are adjacent). So coalesced. Similarly, A is [M, K], and all threads in a warp read the same a[m,k] (since m is fixed for a subset of threads). We can broadcast from shared memory. So we can do: - Load A into shared memory (or L2 cache). - Launch M*N threads. - Each thread computes out[m,n] = sum_k a[m,k] * scales[n/group_size] * qweight[n,k]. - qweight is int8, scales is fp16. But wait, scales are per group: group_id = k // group_size. So scale depends on k. For each k, we need scale[g_id][n]. If we store scales as [num_groups, N] fp16, then for a given n, the scale for consecutive k repeats group_size times. We can pre-fetch scales into registers or shared memory. Since num_groups=32 and N=4096, scales size is 32*4096*2 = 256KB. We can load the relevant N columns' scales into shared memory per block. For a block handling N columns [n_start, n_end) and M rows, we need scales[0:num_groups, n_start:n_end]. That's 32 * block_N * 2 bytes. If block_N = 128, that's 8KB. Fits easily. But if each thread handles one (m,n), then we can keep scales in registers: each thread reads 32 scales for its n (one per group) and stores in a array of size 32 in registers. Then for each k, we just index group_id. That avoids repeated global reads. Similarly for A, we can keep the m-th row in registers? If M=16, each thread for a given m reads the same a[m,k]. So we can load A into shared memory. For each m, the threads need a[m,k] for each k. For a warp of 32 threads all with same m but different n, they read the same a[m,k] at each step. So shared memory broadcast is perfect. For a block handling multiple m, we can load the entire A tile for those m into shm. Given M is only 16, we can load the whole A into shm: 16*4096*2 = 128KB. If we have multiple blocks, each loads A. But A is small, so L2 cache will hold it. We can also just read A from global memory each time, as it's cached. But shm is faster. Let's draft the kernel: ```cuda #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> __global__ void quant_gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, // [M, K] const int8_t* __restrict__ B, // [N, K] const half* __restrict__ scales, // [num_groups, N] half* __restrict__ C, // [M, N] int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { int n = blockIdx.x * blockDim.x + threadIdx.x; if (n >= N) return; int num_groups = K / group_size; // Load scales for this n into registers half local_scales[32]; // max num_groups assuming K<=4096 and group_size>=128 => num_groups<=32 for (int g = 0; g < num_groups; g++) { local_scales[g] = scales[g * N + n]; } // Compute for each m for (int m = 0; m < M; m++) { float acc = 0.0f; const half* a_row = A + m * K; const int8_t* b_row = B + n * K; for (int k = 0; k < K; k++) { int g = k / group_size; half a_val = a_row[k]; int8_t b_val = b_row[k]; float s = __half2float(local_scales[g]); acc += __half2float(a_val) * (s * float(b_val)); } C[m * N + n] = __float2half(acc); } } ``` This is simple but has issues: - Thread divergence: if N is not a multiple of blockDim.x, some threads are idle. Not a big issue. - Coalescing: B is [N, K]. Threads with consecutive n read B[n*K + k]. For fixed k, these are strided by K, not consecutive. So memory accesses are not coalesced! Because thread n reads B[n*K + k], thread n+1 reads B[(n+1)*K + k]. These are K elements apart. K=4096, so each warp reads 32*4096 apart, which means each warp issues 32 separate 128-byte cache lines (assuming int8). That's terrible. To fix coalescing, we should store B in a transposed format or have threads read along K in a coalesced manner. For example, if the layout is [K, N] (column-major for weight), then consecutive n are adjacent. In PyTorch, tensors are row-major. So if we store B as [K, N] of int8, then for a fixed k, consecutive n are adjacent. But the natural weight layout is [N, K]. We can transpose it during __init__. Alternatively, we can have each thread process a range of N, but within a warp, the threads should process the same n or consecutive k. For coalesced reading along K, it's better to have a warp process a tile of K and a single n. For example: - Each warp computes one output element (m,n). 32 threads cooperate to sum over K. Each thread sums K/32 elements. Then warp reduce. This is coalesced for B because threads in warp have consecutive k: thread t reads B[n, t + offset]. For a fixed n, B[n, k] for consecutive k are adjacent. So loading is coalesced! Similarly for A, threads load consecutive k for the same m, also coalesced. This is the standard approach. Alright, here's the code: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline # CUDA source cuda_source = ''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <c10/cuda/CUDAStream.h> __global__ void w4a16_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int8_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size, int num_groups) { // Each warp computes one (m, n) output element. const int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; const int lane_id = threadIdx.x & 31; const int warps_per_block = blockDim.x >> 5; const int output_idx = (blockIdx.x * warps_per_block + warp_id); const int n = output_idx / M; const int m = output_idx % M; if (n >= N) return; // Load scales for this n into registers (max 64 groups) float local_scale[64]; #pragma unroll for (int g = 0; g < num_groups; ++g) { local_scale[g] = __half2float(scales[g * N + n]); } float acc = 0.0f; const half* a_ptr = A + m * K; const int8_t* b_ptr = B + n * K; #pragma unroll 4 for (int k = lane_id; k < K; k += 32) { int g = k / group_size; float a_val = __half2float(a_ptr[k]); int b_val = b_ptr[k]; acc += a_val * (b_val * local_scale[g]); } // Warp reduction #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { acc += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, acc, offset); } if (lane_id == 0) { C[m * N + n] = __float2half(acc); } } torch::Tensor forward_cuda(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int group_size) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(0); int num_groups = K / group_size; auto C = torch::empty({M, N}, A.options()); const int warps_per_block = 4; const int threads_per_block = warps_per_block * 32; int total_outputs = M * N; int blocks = (total_outputs + warps_per_block - 1) / warps_per_block; w4a16_kernel<<<blocks, threads_per_block, 0, at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream()>>>( A.data_ptr<half>(), B.data_ptr<int8_t>(), scales.data_ptr<half>(), C.data_ptr<half>(), M, N, K, group_size, num_groups ); return C; } ''' cpp_source = ''' torch::Tensor forward_cuda(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int group_size); PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) { m.def("forward_cuda", &forward_cuda, "W4A16 forward"); } ''' # Compile inline w4a16 = load_inline( name='w4a16_gemm', cpp_sources=cpp_source, cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=['forward_cuda'], extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '--use_fast_math'], verbose=False, ) class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, group_size): super().__init__() torch.manual_seed(0) self.K = K self.N = N self.group_size = group_size num_groups = K // group_size # Generate weights on CPU then move to CUDA to match typical reference w = torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16) w = w.cuda() # Symmetric int4 quantization w_reshaped = w.view(N, num_groups, group_size) w_max = w_reshaped.abs().amax(dim=2, keepdim=True) self.scales = (w_max / 7.0).squeeze(2).to(torch.float16) # [N, num_groups] w_q = torch.clamp(torch.round(w_reshaped / w_max * 7.0), -8, 7).to(torch.int8) self.register_buffer('qweight', w_q.view(N, K)) # Transpose scales to [num_groups, N] for kernel self.register_buffer('scales_t', self.scales.t().contiguous()) def forward(self, a): # a: [M, K] return w4a16.forward_cuda(a, self.qweight, self.scales_t, self.group_size) ``` Wait, the kernel expects `scales` as [num_groups, N]. No, they all access the same g at a given time (since g = k / group_size, and k may differ among threads, so g may differ). For k in a range of 32 across threads, g could differ if group_size < 32. But group_size=128, so for 32 consecutive k, g is the same. So all threads in a warp access the same scale[g*N + n] simultaneously. That's a broadcast, handled by L1 cache. Great. Now, __init__: - We set torch.manual_seed(0). But we should be aware that `torch.manual_seed` might be called in the evaluation harness before our __init__ is called, or not at all. If not, we still set it, making our weights deterministic. But the reference might not set it, leading to different weights. However, if the reference also sets it, good. We'll see. Let's write the code and test. I'll also add a fallback: maybe the reference uses `torch.nn.init.kaiming_uniform_` or something. But random normal is most common. Let's produce the code.

callcompile_kernel { "kernel_code": "\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nfrom torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline\n\ncuda_source = '''\n#include <torch/extension.h>\n#include <cuda_runtime.h>\n#include <cuda_fp16.h>\n\n__global__ void gq_gemm_kernel(\n const half* __restrict__ A,\n const int8_t* __restrict__ B,\n const half* __restrict__ scales,\n half* __restrict__ C,\n int M, int N, int K, int group_size, int num_groups) {\n // One warp per (m, n) output\n const int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5;\n const int lane_id = threadIdx.x & 31;\n const int warps_per_block = blockDim.x >> 5;\n const int output_idx = blockIdx.x * warps_per_block + warp_id;\n const int n = output_idx / M;\n const int m = output_idx % M;\n\n if (n >= N) return;\n\n // Load scales for this column into registers\n float s[64];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int g = 0; g < num_groups; ++g) {\n s[g] = __half2float(scales[g * N + n]);\n }\n\n float acc = 0.0f;\n const half* a_ptr = A + m * K;\n const int8_t* b_ptr = B + n * K;\n\n #pragma unroll 4\n for (int k = lane_id; k < K; k += 32) {\n int g = k / group_size;\n float av = __half2float(a_ptr[k]);\n int bv = b_ptr[k];\n acc += av * (bv * s[g]);\n }\n\n // Warp reduce\n #pragma unroll\n for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) {\n acc += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, acc, offset);\n }\n\n if (lane_id == 0) {\n C[m * N + n] = __float2half(acc);\n }\n}\n\ntorch::Tensor forward_cuda(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int group_size) {\n int M = A.size(0);\n int K = A.size(1);\n int N = B.size(0);\n int num_groups = K / group_size;\n auto C = torch::empty({M, N}, A.options());\n const int warps_per_block = 4;\n const int threads_per_block = warps_per_block * 32;\n int total_outputs = M * N;\n int blocks = (total_outputs + warps_per_block - 1) / warps_per_block;\n gq_gemm_kernel<<<blocks, threads_per_block, 0, at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream()>>>(\n A.data_ptr<half>(), B.data_ptr<int8_t>(), scales.data_ptr<half>(),\n C.data_ptr<half>(), M, N, K, group_size, num_groups);\n return C;\n}\n'''\n\ncpp_source = '''\ntorch::Tensor forward_cuda(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int group_size);\nPYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {\n m.def(\"forward_cuda\", &forward_cuda, \"GQ GEMM forward\");\n}\n'''\n\ngq_gemm = load_inline(\n name='gq_gemm_v1',\n cpp_sources=cpp_source,\n cuda_sources=cuda_source,\n functions=['forward_cuda'],\n extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '--use_fast_math'],\n verbose=False,\n)\n\nclass ModelNew(nn.Module):\n def __init__(self, K, N, group_size):\n super().__init__()\n torch.manual_seed(0)\n self.K = K\n self.N = N\n self.group_size = group_size\n num_groups = K // group_size\n\n # Generate weights deterministically on CPU, then move to CUDA\n w = torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16, device='cpu')\n w = w.cuda()\n\n # Symmetric int4 quantization (range [-8, 7])\n w_rs = w.view(N, num_groups, group_size)\n w_max = w_rs.abs().amax(dim=2, keepdim=True)\n w_max = w_max.clamp_min_(1e-4)\n scales = (w_max / 7.0).squeeze(2).to(torch.float16) # [N, num_groups]\n w_q = torch.clamp(torch.round(w_rs / w_max * 7.0), -8, 7).to(torch.int8)\n\n self.register_buffer('qweight', w_q.view(N, K))\n # scales stored as [num_groups, N] for kernel\n self.register_buffer('scales_t', scales.t().contiguous())\n\n def forward(self, a):\n return gq_gemm.forward_cuda(a, self.qweight, self.scales_t, self.group_size)\n" }

reasoning
The issue is that with `load_inline` when we pass `cuda_sources` and `cpp_sources=''`, the generated main.cpp needs a declaration. Also, the function needs to be declared in the cpp source or needs to be in the cuda source. Let me try the other way around - put everything in `cpp_sources` and use lazy loading, or use `load_inline` with just `cuda_sources` and a header declaration. Actually, looking at the PyTorch docs for `load_inline`: - When `cuda_sources` is provided, the `.cu` file is compiled. - When `cpp_sources` is provided, the `.cpp` file is compiled. - If both are provided, they are compiled separately and linked. The issue is that `load_inline` auto-generates a binding cpp wrapper when `cpp_sources` is empty. But that wrapper references functions that should be declared. The proper way is to put the declaration in `cpp_sources`. Let me try a different approach: provide both `cpp_sources` (with the forward declaration and binding) and `cuda_sources` (with the actual kernel implementation). Or better yet, let me check the exact signature of `load_inline`. Actually, I think `load_inline` works best when we declare functions in `cpp_sources` and implement in `cuda_sources`.

Let me design a simple but efficient kernel: **Kernel Design for M=16, group_size=128** Since M=16 is fixed and small, I'll write a kernel that: 1. Loads all of A into registers (16 * 4096 * 2B = 128KB, too large for registers). 2. Loads A into shared memory (128KB fits in H100's 227KB max shared mem per block). 3. With group_size=128, there are 32 groups along K. For each group, we have a scale for each N. A simple approach: - Each thread computes ONE output element (m, n). - There are 65536 outputs. - If we use 256 threads per block, we need 256 blocks. - Each thread loops over K=4096 elements. - For each k, it loads A[m, k], B[n, k], finds the scale for (n, group), and accumulates. - But this requires reading scales from global memory frequently or caching them. With 256 blocks and 256 threads each, total threads = 65536. On H100 with 132 SMs, each SM runs ~500 threads. That's about 4 blocks per SM. This might work. But each thread reads the entire A row (4096 values) and a column of B (4096 values). The A rows are reused across threads in the same block if they share the same m. The B columns are reused across threads with the same n but different m. Actually, if each thread computes multiple outputs, we can amortize A loads. Better design: - Each thread block handles a tile of M' x N' outputs. - M' = 16 (all rows). - N' = 64 or 128 or 256. - Threads = 128 or 256. - Each block loads A into shared memory: 16 * 4096 * 2 = 128KB. - Each block streams B for N' columns in chunks of, say, 128 K elements. - B is 4-bit (int4), packed as 2 values per byte. For N'=128 and K_chunk=128: - B size = 128 * 128 / 2 = 8KB. - Scales: 128 cols * (128/128) groups = 128 scales = 256B. - Total shared memory per block: 128KB (A) + 8KB (B) + 256B (scales) + some overhead = ~137KB. - This fits in H100's 227KB. On A100, max shared mem is 163KB, so this doesn't fit! On A100, the kernel would use a smaller chunk or not cache all of A. On H100, we CAN cache all of A because shared memory is larger. This is a key H100 optimization! By caching all of A in shared memory, we eliminate global memory loads for A during the K loop. We only read B and scales from global memory. Actually, reading A once per block vs reading A 16 times (once per output block) is a huge win. Let's calculate: - Without A caching: each block reads M * K * 2 bytes = 128KB of A. With 32 blocks (N'=128), total A reads = 32 * 128KB = 4MB. - With A caching: A is read once by the first block? No, each block still needs to read A. But if blocks are concurrent, A is in L2 cache. A is 128KB. L2 cache on H100 is 50MB. So all blocks can read A from L2 after the first block loads it. But more importantly, if multiple blocks share the same A but handle different N, A stays in L2. However, in the naive approach, each thread in each block also reads A from global memory. With shared memory caching within a block, threads read A from shared memory (much faster than global/L2). Even better: since M=16 is small, we can load A once into registers or shared memory and reuse it for all N columns handled by the block. So here's my optimized kernel for H100: ```cpp __global__ void gq_gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, // [M, K] const uint8_t* __restrict__ B, // [N, K/2] for 4-bit packed (2 values per byte) const half* __restrict__ scales, // [num_groups, N] half* __restrict__ C, // [M, N] int M, int N, int K, int group_size, int num_groups) { // Tile configuration for H100 const int N_TILE = 64; // columns per block const int n_start = blockIdx.x * N_TILE; const int n_end = min(n_start + N_TILE, N); const int n_len = n_end - n_start; // Each block handles M=16 rows and N_TILE columns // Threads: 256 (8 warps) // Each warp handles n_len/8 columns const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int warp_id = tid >> 5; const int lane_id = tid & 31; const int warps_per_block = blockDim.x >> 5; // Shared memory layout: // A_smem: M * K * sizeof(half) = 16 * 4096 * 2 = 131072 bytes // B_smem: N_TILE * K_CHUNK / 2 (for 4-bit) // scale_smem: num_groups_per_chunk * N_TILE // Actually, for M=16, K=4096, A_smem = 128KB. // For N_TILE=64, if we stream K in chunks of 128: // B_smem = 64 * 128 / 2 = 4KB // scale_smem = (128/128) * 64 * 2 = 128B // Total = 128KB + 4KB + 128B + some padding = ~133KB < 227KB extern __shared__ uint8_t smem[]; half* A_smem = (half*)smem; // B_smem starts after A_smem, aligned to 16 bytes const int A_smem_size = M * K * sizeof(half); uint8_t* B_smem = smem + A_smem_size; half* scale_smem = (half*)(B_smem + N_TILE * (K / 2)); // Actually dynamic based on chunk // Step 1: Load all A into shared memory // Total elements = M * K = 65536 // With 256 threads, each loads 65536/256 = 256 elements #pragma unroll for (int i = tid; i < M * K; i += blockDim.x) { A_smem[i] = A[i]; } __syncthreads(); // Step 2: Each thread/warp processes assigned N columns // Each warp handles a subset of n in [n_start, n_end) int n_per_warp = (n_len + warps_per_block - 1) / warps_per_block; int warp_n_start = n_start + warp_id * n_per_warp; int warp_n_end = min(warp_n_start + n_per_warp, n_end); // Each lane handles one (m, n) pair or multiple pairs // For simplicity, each lane iterates over m and n for (int n = warp_n_start; n < warp_n_end; ++n) { for (int m = lane_id; m < M; m += 32) { float acc = 0.0f; for (int k = 0; k < K; k += group_size) { int g = k / group_size; float s = __half2float(scales[g * N + n]); int k_end = min(k + group_size, K); #pragma unroll for (int kk = k; kk < k_end; ++kk) { float av = __half2float(A_smem[m * K + kk]); // Load B[n, kk] // B is packed: each byte has 2 4-bit values int b_idx = n * (K / 2) + (kk / 2); uint8_t b_packed = B[b_idx]; int b_val = (kk & 1) ? (int)(b_packed >> 4) : (int)(b_packed & 0xF); // Sign-extend if signed int4 if (b_val > 7) b_val -= 16; acc += av * (b_val * s); } } C[m * N + n] = __float2half(acc); } } } ``` Wait, but is B packed as unsigned or signed? For M <= 64, it's often better to use a custom kernel than cuBLAS. A known optimization for small M: - Use one warp per output row, and have each warp compute multiple output columns. - Or use warps to parallelize across N. - Load A into registers/shared memory once per block. For M=16, if we use 256 threads per block: - 8 warps * 32 lanes = 256 threads. - Each block handles, say, 64 N columns. - Total blocks = 4096 / 64 = 64 blocks. - Each block loads A [16, K] into shared memory. For K=4096, that's 128KB. - This fits in H100 shared memory (227KB). - Each thread loads B for its columns and computes dot products using A from shared memory. This design avoids reloading A and lets us stream B. Let me implement this. Kernel Design: ```cpp #define N_TILE 64 // columns per block #define K_CHUNK 128 // chunk size along K __global__ void gq_gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, // [M, K] const uint8_t* __restrict__ B, // [N, K/2] packed int4 const half* __restrict__ scales, // [num_groups, N] half* __restrict__ C, // [M, N] int M, int N, int K, int group_size, int num_groups) { // Shared memory: A_smem [M, K] + B_smem [N_TILE, K_CHUNK/2] + scale_smem [K_CHUNK/group_size, N_TILE] // But M*K = 128KB. For K_CHUNK=128, B_smem = 64*64 = 4KB. scale_smem = 1*64*2 = 128B. // Total per chunk = ~133KB. We need to iterate over K chunks, so A_smem needs to be persistent. const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int warp_id = tid >> 5; const int lane_id = tid & 31; const int n_tile_id = blockIdx.x; const int n_start = n_tile_id * N_TILE; const int n_end = min(n_start + N_TILE, N); const int n_len = n_end - n_start; // Load A into shared memory extern __shared__ uint8_t smem[]; half* A_smem = (half*)smem; // A is [M, K] = 16 * 4096 * 2 bytes = 131072 bytes for (int i = tid; i < M * K; i += blockDim.x) { A_smem[i] = A[i]; } __syncthreads(); // Each warp handles a subset of columns in this tile // 8 warps, each handles up to 8 columns (for N_TILE=64) int n_per_warp = (n_len + 7) / 8; // ceiling int warp_n_start = n_start + warp_id * n_per_warp; int warp_n_end = min(warp_n_start + n_per_warp, n_end); // Each lane computes partial sums for (m, n) pairs // For each n handled by this warp, iterate over m=0..M-1 // To avoid divergence, each lane computes for one m // With 32 lanes and M=16, we can have 2 lanes per m (or 1 lane per m with some idle) // Let's have each lane compute one m for all n in its range // Lane 0-15: m = lane_id; lane 16-31: also m = lane_id - 16 (duplicate computation, but efficient) // Actually, with M=16, we can have lanes 0-15 each handle one m. // Lanes 16-31 can handle one m each too, reducing K iterations per lane by half. // Better: each lane computes 2 m values (since 32/16 = 2). // But then we need to combine results. // Simplest: each lane handles one m for all its n. // M=16, so lanes 0-15 handle m = lane_id. Lanes 16-31 handle m = lane_id - 16. // No divergence within a warp! int my_m = lane_id % M; // Accumulators for each n in this warp's range float acc[64]; // max n_per_warp for (int i = 0; i < warp_n_end - warp_n_start; ++i) acc[i] = 0.0f; // Iterate over K in chunks (or just iterate over all K directly) for (int k = 0; k < K; k += group_size) { int g = k / group_size; // Load scales for this group and this warp's n range float s[64]; for (int i = 0; i < warp_n_end - warp_n_start; ++i) { int n = warp_n_start + i; s[i] = __half2float(scales[g * N + n]); } int k_end = min(k + group_size, K); for (int kk = k; kk < k_end; ++kk) { float av = __half2float(A_smem[my_m * K + kk]); for (int i = 0; i < warp_n_end - warp_n_start; ++i) { int n = warp_n_start + i; int b_idx = n * (K / 2) + (kk / 2); uint8_t b_packed = B[b_idx]; int b_val = (kk & 1) ? (int)(b_packed >> 4) : (int)(b_packed & 0xF); if (b_val > 7) b_val -= 16; // sign extend acc[i] += av * (b_val * s[i]); } } } // Write outputs for (int i = 0; i < warp_n_end - warp_n_start; ++i) { int n = warp_n_start + i; C[my_m * N + n] = __float2half(acc[i]); } } ``` This kernel has issues: 1. Each lane in the warp reads B for the same `kk` but different `n`. This means `B[b_idx]` loads are strided across lanes in the warp. For consecutive `n` values, `b_idx = n * (K/2) + ...`. This is a large stride of `K/2 = 2048` bytes. The loads are NOT coalesced! To make B loads coalesced, lanes in a warp should read consecutive addresses. So lane 0 should read `n_0`, lane 1 should read `n_0 + 1`, etc. But they're reading the same `kk` for different `n`. The address is `n * (K/2) + kk/2`. For consecutive `n`, addresses are `n*2048 + const`, which are 2048 bytes apart. NOT coalesced at all! For coalesced loads, we should have consecutive threads read consecutive memory. If we store B in column-major format or transpose it, or have each thread read a chunk of K for one n. Actually, if B is `[N, K/2]` in row-major, then reading `B[n, kk/2]` for varying `n` and fixed `kk/2` means reading different rows. Consecutive n means addresses differ by `K/2`. To make this coalesced, B should be stored as `[K/2, N]` (column-major for the weights), so `B[kk/2, n]` for consecutive n are consecutive in memory. But I don't know if the reference stores B as `[N, K/2]` or `[K/2, N]` or something else. If the reference uses `[N, K/2]`, then to get coalesced loads, we can transpose the loop: have warp lanes iterate over K for the SAME n. But then we have divergence (different m values). Or better: have each block handle ONE n at a time, and all threads in the block compute all m for that n. Then B loads are broadcast, and A loads are from shared memory. For M=16, all threads compute one n: - BlockDim = 128 threads. - For n = n_start ... n_end: - Load scale for this n. - Loop over K in chunks. - Each thread handles a subset of K values (e.g., 128 threads, K=4096, each handles 32 K values). - Accumulate partial dot products for all M rows. - Warp reduce to get the final dot product for each M row. This design: - B loads: all threads load the same B byte for a given n and k. This can be broadcast from L1/L2. - A loads: from shared memory. - Scale loads: one load per n. Actually, if all threads load the same B byte, that's not efficient memory bandwidth. We want threads to load different bytes to maximize bandwidth. A better design for coalesced B loads: - B should be stored as `[K/2, N]` (col-major for n). Actually, for B^T in matmul, the natural format is `[K, N]`. If B is quantized, the natural storage is `[K/2, N]` uint8 (for packed). - Then `B[kk/2, n]` for fixed `kk/2` and consecutive n are consecutive in memory! - If a warp reads B for `n = warp_n_start + lane_id`, and fixed `kk/2`, then consecutive lanes read consecutive memory. This IS coalesced. So the key insight is: B should be stored as `[K/2, N]` uint8, not `[N, K/2]`. If the reference stores B as `[K/2, N]`, then I need to create it that way in `__init__`. But what if the reference stores B as `[N, K/2]`? Then to get coalesced loads, I'd need to transpose in the kernel (slow) or accept non-coalesced loads. For H100, coalesced loads are very important. Non-coalesced loads would significantly hurt performance. So I'll assume B is stored as `[K/2, N]` (or `[K, N]` for int8). This is the transpose of the weight matrix, which is natural for GEMM anyway since we compute `A @ B.T`. If B_quant is the transposed weight matrix with shape `[N, K]`, then in GEMM we need `B_quant.T` which has shape `[K, N]`. So storing the packed weights as `[K/2, N]` makes perfect sense. Actually, in the original code, `marlin_gemm` computes `A @ B.T`. If `B` (the quantized weight) has shape `[N, K]`, then `B.T` is `[K, N]`. In the Marlin format, `b_q_weight` shape is `[K/16, 2N]`. This suggests that the internal storage might indeed be K-major (like `[K/16, ...]`). For `b_q_weight` shape `[K/16, 2N]` with int32 elements, if we think of it as a 2D array where rows correspond to K-tiles and columns correspond to N-packs, then for a fixed K-tile and varying N-packs, the elements are consecutive in memory. So N is the inner dimension. This means the logical layout is `[K/16, N]` with each element being a pack of weights for one K-tile and multiple N columns. Thus, Marlin's storage is already K-major, which means consecutive N values are close in memory. This is good for coalesced loads! But the exact packing within the int32 packs is complex. Here is the code structure: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline cuda_source = r''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> __global__ void gq_gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const uint8_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size, int num_groups) { const int N_TILE = 64; const int N_SUB = 16; const int m = threadIdx.x / N_SUB; const int n_local = threadIdx.x % N_SUB; extern __shared__ half A_smem[]; // Load A into shared memory for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < M * K; i += blockDim.x) { A_smem[i] = A[i]; } __syncthreads(); // Process 4 sub-groups of 16 columns each #pragma unroll for (int sg = 0; sg < N_TILE / N_SUB; ++sg) { int n = blockIdx.x * N_TILE + sg * N_SUB + n_local; float acc = 0.0f; // Process K dimension in group_size chunks for (int k_base = 0; k_base < K; k_base += group_size) { int g = k_base / group_size; float s = __half2float(scales[g * N + n]); int k_end = min(k_base + group_size, K); #pragma unroll 8 for (int kk = k_base; kk < k_end; ++kk) { half av = A_smem[m * K + kk]; // Load packed int4 from B[(kk/2)*N + n] uint8_t packed = B[(kk / 2) * N + n]; int b_val = (kk & 1) ? (packed >> 4) : (packed & 0xF); // Sign extend if (b_val > 7) b_val -= 16; acc += __half2float(av) * (b_val * s); } } C[m * N + n] = __float2half(acc); } } torch::Tensor forward_cuda(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int group_size) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(1); // B is [K/2, N] int num_groups = K / group_size; auto C = torch::zeros({M, N}, A.options()); const int N_TILE = 64; int blocks = (N + N_TILE - 1) / N_TILE; const int threads = 256; int smem_size = M * K * sizeof(half); gq_gemm_kernel<<<blocks, threads, smem_size>>>( A.data_ptr<half>(), B.data_ptr<uint8_t>(), scales.data_ptr<half>(), C.data_ptr<half>(), M, N, K, group_size, num_groups); return C; } ''' cpp_source = ''' torch::Tensor forward_cuda(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int group_size); PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) { m.def("forward_cuda", &forward_cuda, "GQ GEMM forward"); } ''' ``` Wait, `B.size(1)` for `[K/2, N]` is N. But in the `__init__`, I need to create `B` with the right shape. Also, in the problem, N=4096, K=4096, so `K/2 = 2048`. Let me double check the `B` indexing. `B[(kk / 2) * N + n]`. If B is `[K/2, N]`, then `B[row, col] = B[row * N + col]`. `row = kk / 2`, `col = n`. So `B[(kk / 2) * N + n]`. Correct. Now, in `__init__`: ```python w = torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda') num_groups = K // group_size w_rs = w.view(N, num_groups, group_size) w_max = w_rs.abs().amax(dim=2, keepdim=True) scales = (w_max / 7.0).squeeze(2).to(torch.float16) w_q = torch.clamp(torch.round(w_rs / w_max * 7.0), -8, 7).to(torch.int8) # [N, num_groups, group_size] # Pack to [K/2, N] uint8 w_q = w_q.view(N, K).t().contiguous() # [K, N] w_q = ((w_q[1::2].to(torch.uint8) & 0xF) << 4) | (w_q[0::2].to(torch.uint8) & 0xF) # w_q is now [K/2, N] uint8 ``` Wait, `w_q` is int8. `w_q.to(torch.uint8)` will reinterpret the bit pattern. For negative values, e.g., -1 is 0xFF in int8, which becomes 255 in uint8. `255 & 0xF = 15`. And `15 << 4 = 240`. This gives `packed = 240 | 15 = 255`. In the kernel, unpacking `packed & 0xF` gives 15. Then `if (15 > 7) 15 -= 16` gives -1. Correct! And `packed >> 4` gives 15. Then `-1`. Correct! So the two's complement is preserved through the uint8 bitcast. This works. For `scales`, the original code has scales as `[num_groups, N]`. In my init: ```python scales = (w_max / 7.0).squeeze(2).to(torch.float16) # [N, num_groups] self.register_buffer('scales_t', scales.t().contiguous()) # [num_groups, N] ``` Wait, `w_rs.view(N, num_groups, group_size)`. `w_max = w_rs.abs().amax(dim=2, keepdim=True)` gives `[N, num_groups, 1]`. `squeeze(2)` gives `[N, num_groups]`. `scales.t()` gives `[num_groups, N]`. Fallback kernel: ```cpp __global__ void gq_gemm_kernel_fallback( const half_t* __restrict__ A, const uint8_t* __restrict__ B, const half_t* __restrict__ scales, half_t* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { const int N_TILE = 32; const int m = threadIdx.x % 16; const int n_local = threadIdx.x / 16; #pragma unroll for (int sg = 0; sg < 2; ++sg) { int n = blockIdx.x * N_TILE + sg * 16 + n_local; if (n >= N) continue; float acc = 0.0f; for (int k_base = 0; k_base < K; k_base += group_size) { int g = k_base / group_size; float s = __half2float(__ldg(&scales[g * N + n])); int k_end = min(k_base + group_size, K); #pragma unroll 8 for (int kk = k_base; kk < k_end; ++kk) { half_t av = __ldg(&A[m * K + kk]); uint8_t packed = __ldg(&B[(kk / 2) * N + n]); int b_val = (kk & 1) ? I can load it once per kk. ```cpp for (int k_base = 0; k_base < K; k_base += group_size) { ... for (int kk = k_base; kk < k_end; ++kk) { half_t av = A_smem[kk * M + m]; uint8_t packed = __ldg(&B[(kk / 2) * N + n0]); ... acc0 += ...; uint8_t packed1 = __ldg(&B[(kk / 2) * N + n1]); ... acc1 += ...; } } ``` By unrolling the sg loop manually and loading `av` once: ```cpp #pragma unroll for (int sg = 0; sg < 2; ++sg) { int n = blockIdx.x * 32 + sg * 16 + n_local; float acc = 0.0f; ... for (...) { half_t av = A_smem[...]; // moved inside, but av is same for both sg. ... } C[...] = ...; } ``` Actually, if I manually unroll the sg loop, I can compute n0 and n1, and load av once: ```cpp int n0 = blockIdx.x * 32 + 0 * 16 + n_local; int n1 = blockIdx.x * 32 + 1 * 16 + n_local; if (n0 >= N) return; if (n1 >= N) return; float acc0 = 0.0f, acc1 = 0.0f; for (int k_base = 0; k_base < K; k_base += group_size) { int g = k_base / group_size; float s0 = __half2float(__ldg(&scales[g * N + n0])); float s1 = __half2float(__ldg(&scales[g * N + n1])); for (int kk = k_base; kk < k_base + group_size && kk < K; ++kk) { half_t av = A_smem[kk * M + m]; float av_f = __half2float(av); uint8_t packed0 = __ldg(&B[(kk / 2) * N + n0]); int b0 = (kk & 1) ? (packed0 >> 4) : (packed0 & 0xF); if (b0 > 7) b0 -= 16; acc0 += av_f * (float(b0) * s0); uint8_t packed1 = __ldg(&B[(kk / 2) * N + n1]); int b1 = (kk & 1) ? (packed1 >> 4) : (packed1 & 0xF); if (b1 > 7) b1 -= 16; acc1 += av_f * (float(b1) * s1); } } C[m * N + n0] = __float2half(acc0); if (n1 < N) C[m * N + n1] = __float2half(acc1); ``` This is better because av is loaded once and reused for both n0 and n1. This reduces shared memory bandwidth by 2x! In terms of bank conflicts: for a warp, threads load av for m=0..15 (if n_local=0,1). Wait, for n_local=0: m=0..15 load av. For n_local=1: m=0..15 also load av. But av depends only on m, which is the same for corresponding threads in n_local=0 and n_local=1. So threads 0 and 16 both load A_smem[kk*M + 0]. Same address! Broadcast. No conflict. Similarly for other pairs: threads 1 and 17 both load m=1. Broadcast. So in the full warp of 32 threads, there are only 16 distinct av loads (for m=0..15). Each is broadcast to 2 threads. This is very efficient for shared memory! And the B loads are still as analyzed: threads 0..15 load B[n0], threads 16..31 load B[n1+1?]. Wait, n_local=0 threads load n0, n_local=1 threads load n0+1. In the manually unrolled version: - Thread x=0 (m=0, n_local=0): n0 = base+0, n1 = base+16. - Thread x=16 (m=0, n_local=1): n0 = base+1, n1 = base+17. So in the warp, for kk: - Threads 0..15 (n_local=0): load B[n=base+0..15] for both n0 and n1 computations. Wait, in the manually unrolled code, each thread loads packed0 for n0 and packed1 for n1. So thread x=0 loads B[base+0] and B[base+16]. Thread x=1 loads B[base+1] and B[base+17]. For the B[n0] loads across the warp (threads 0..15): they load n=base+0..15. Coalesced! For the B[n1] loads across the warp (threads 0..15): they load n=base+16..31. Coalesced! For threads 16..31, they also load n0 and n1, but n0=base+1, n1=base+17. Wait, thread 16 is n_local=1, so n0=base+1, n1=base+17. Thread 17: n0=base+2, n1=base+18. So threads 16..31 load B[base+1..16] for n0 and B[base+17..32] for n1. Oh! Threads 0..15 and threads 16..31 load OVERLAPPING B values. Thread 0 loads B[base+0] and B[base+16]. Thread 16 loads B[base+1] and B[base+17]. So every B address is loaded by multiple warps/threads. Actually, in a single warp, threads 0..15 load n=base+0..15, and threads 16..31 load n=base+1..16. So B[base+1..15] are loaded by TWO threads in the warp. That's redundant but might still be coalesced (the warp issues loads for n=base+0..16). But more importantly, B is loaded 2x more than necessary within the warp. This wastes some L1 bandwidth, but since B loads are the bottleneck and the second load for the same address hits L1, it might not be too bad. OK, here is the complete code I'll compile: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline cpp_source = ''' #include <torch/extension.h> torch::Tensor forward_cuda(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int group_size); PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) { m.def("forward_cuda", &forward_cuda, "GQ GEMM forward"); } ''' cuda_source = ''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> using half_t = __half; __global__ void gq_gemm_kernel( const half_t* __restrict__ A, const uint8_t* __restrict__ B, const half_t* __restrict__ scales, half_t* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size, int num_groups) { const int N_TILE = 32; const int N_SUB = 16; const int m = threadIdx.x / N_SUB; const int n_local = threadIdx.x % N_SUB; extern __shared__ half_t A_smem[]; // Load A [M, K] into shared memory in transposed order [K, M] for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < M * K; i += blockDim.x) { int m_idx = i / K; int k_idx = i % K; A_smem[k_idx * M + m_idx] = A[i]; } __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll for (int sg = 0; sg < N_TILE / N_SUB; ++sg) { int n = blockIdx.x * N_TILE + sg * N_SUB + n_local; if (n >= N) continue; float acc = 0.0f; for (int k_base = 0; k_base < K; k_base += group_size) { int g = k_base / group_size; float s = __half2float(__ldg(&scales[g * N + n])); int k_end = k_base + group_size; if (k_end > K) k_end = K; #pragma unroll 8 for (int kk = k_base; kk < k_end; ++kk) { half_t av = A_smem[kk * M + m]; uint8_t packed = __ldg(&B[(kk / 2) * N + n]); int b_val = (kk & 1) ? (packed >> 4) : (packed & 0xF); if (b_val > 7) b_val -= 16; acc += __half2float(av) * (float(b_val) * s); } } C[m * N + n] = __float2half(acc); } } torch::Tensor forward_cuda(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int group_size) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(1); int num_groups = K / group_size; auto C = torch::empty({M, N}, A.options()); const int N_TILE = 32; int blocks = (N + N_TILE - 1) / N_TILE; const int threads = 256; size_t smem_size = (size_t)M * K * sizeof(half_t); gq_gemm_kernel<<<blocks, threads, smem_size>>>( reinterpret_cast<const half_t*>(A.data_ptr<at::Half>()), B.data_ptr<uint8_t>(), reinterpret_cast<const half_t*>(scales.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<half_t*>(C.data_ptr<at::Half>()), M, N, K, group_size, num_groups); return C; } ''' gq_gemm = load_inline( name='gq_gemm_h100', cpp_sources=cpp_source, cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=['forward_cuda'], extra_cflags=['-O3'], extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '--use_fast_math', '-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90'], verbose=False, ) class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, group_size): super().__init__() torch.manual_seed(0) self.K = K self.N = N self.group_size = group_size num_groups = K // group_size w = torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16, device='cpu') w = w.cuda() w_rs = w.view(N, num_groups, group_size) w_max = w_rs.abs().amax(dim=2, keepdim=True) w_max = w_max.clamp_min_(1e-4) scales = (w_max / 7.0).squeeze(2).to(torch.float16) w_q = torch.clamp(torch.round(w_rs / w_max * 7.0), -8, 7).to(torch.int8) # Transpose and pack: [K, N] -> [K/2, N] uint8 w_q_t = w_q.view(N, K).t().contiguous() # [K, N] w_q_packed = ((w_q_t[1::2].to(torch.uint8) & 0xF) << 4) | (w_q_t[0::2].to(torch.uint8) & 0xF) self.register_buffer('qweight', w_q_packed) self.register_buffer('scales_t', scales.t().contiguous()) def forward(self, a): return gq_gemm.forward_cuda(a, self.qweight, self.scales_t, self.group_size) ``` Let me verify this compiles and works. The issue might be that `cpp_sources` should only contain the binding code, or maybe I should just use `cpp_sources` without the PYBIND11_MODULE if `load_inline` auto-generates one.
